                                                  DISCLAIMER
         Guidelines and Protocols are there to assist doctors with their decision-making.
     However, please note that they are not intended to replace clinical judgement.

        We have made every effort to have accurate and the most up to date, evidence-
based information, but we do not take responsibility for any adverse events arising from

                                        mistakes or omissions.

        This document is updated from time-to-time so please ensure that you have the
                                       most up-to-date version.

          ACETYLCHOLINESTERASE INHIBITOR (AChE-i) OVERDOSE
                      (ORGANOPHOSPHATE/CARBAMATE)

                                       SYMPTOMS & SIGNS

                MUSCARINIC ("DUMBELS")                         NICOTINIC           CNS

          D - diaphoresis, diarrhoea                     Fasciculations   Anxiety
          U - urination                                  Weakness         Ataxia
          M - miosis                                     Hypertension     Restlessness
          B - bradycardia, bronchorrhoea,                Tachycardia      Confusion
                                                         Mydriasis        Convulsions
                  bronchospasm                                            Coma
          E - emesis
          L - lacrimation
          S - salivation

                                      IMMEDIATE MANAGEMENT

          * Universal precautions
          * Decontamination - remove clothing, wash patient with soap and water
          * Supportive care of ABCs and general management principles of a poisoned

           patient (see protocol)
                                                                Note:

                If intubation is required, avoid succinylcholine if possible (causes prolonged paralysis)

                               SPECIFIC MANAGEMENT

** ATROPINE
     - can be administered IM if no immediate IV access
     - this only treats the muscarinic symptoms

          STAT DOSE                                          INFUSION (start once atropinised)

          0.02 - 0.05mg/kg IV                                             0.02 - 0.08 mg/kg/hr

          double every 5 minutes until atropinisation        20mg Atropine in 200ml 0.9% NaCl (0.1mg/ml)

          "escalating atropinisation"                        Use a Dial-a-flow/Infusion pump

                                                                          or

                                                             10-20% of total dose already given/hr

                                                             (total dose i.e. for escalating atropinisation)

          END POINT = CLEARING OF RESPIRATORY SECRETIONS (ATROPINISATION) (NOT based on tachycardia or pupil dilation)

** SEIZURES
     - Check blood glucose
     - Use a benzodiazepine only - NO anticonvulsant agents
                - Clonazepam; Diazepam; Lorazepam

** MONITOR & CORRECT ELECTROLYTE ABNORMALITIES

** WEAKNESS
- look for signs of weakness (nicotinic symptom)

          - is the patient able to lift and hold their head up for 10 seconds; ask the patient to squeeze your hand
- Intubation and supportive management is the only way to control weakness (respiratory failure)

                                          DISPOSITION
                                       *GIT Consultation

                                                             Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

TIME IS MUSCLE - do not delay reperfusion

                                                                                                                                          - 1mm in all other leads

                                                                                                                                          - 1.5mm in leads V2 and V3

                                                                              SGARBOSSA CRITERIA

                                                                                     LBBB or PACED RHYTHM
                                                                    * Criteria A (5 points)

                                                                       1mm of concordant ST elevation in at least one lead
                                                                    * Criteria B (3 points)

                                                                       1mm of concordant ST depression in lead V1,V2 or V3
                                                                    * Criteria C (2 points)

                                                                       5mm of discordant ST elevation in at least one lead

                                                                           A total score of  3 has a specificity of 90% for
                                                                                   diagnosing myocardial infarction

              Absolute Contraindications Thrombolysis                     Relative Contraindications Thrombolysis

          * Any prior intracranial haemorrhage                      * History of chronic, severe, poorly controlled hypertension
          * Known structural cerebral vascular lesion e.g. AV       * SBP > 180mmHg or DBP > 110 mmHg - lower BP with

           Malformation                                              Nitroglycerine (Nitrocine�) infusion
          * Known malignant intracranial neoplasm                   * Traumatic or prolonged CPR (> 10min) or major surgery
          * Ischaemic stroke within 3 months except acute
                                                                     (< 3 weeks)
           ischaemic stroke within 3 hours                          * Recent (within 2-4 weeks) internal bleeding
          * Suspected aortic dissection                             * Non-compressible vascular punctures
          * Active bleeding or bleeding diathesis                   * Pregnancy
          * Significant closed head trauma or facial trauma within  * Active peptic ulcer disease (bled within past 2-4 weeks)
                                                                    * Current use of anticoagulants e.g. Warfarin
           3 months                                                 * For Streptokinase: prior exposure or prior allergic reaction

                                                                                                                                Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

                  STEMI EQUIVALENTS

ECG Patterns Hinting for Potentially High-Risk Critical Artery Stenosis or Occlusion

                          (A) Conventional STEMI (B-K) Potential STEMI Equivalents

                                                        STEMI EQUIVALENTS
                               *Those patients who do not present with the classical
                                ECG changes but have acutely occluded coronary artery/s
                               *Often associated with poorer outcome and worse prognosis
                               *Benefit from timely intervention

https://www.jacc.org/doi/full/10.1016/j.jaccas.2019.10.030  Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

===== PAGE 28 =====
Contents

                                    Components of Post-Cardiac Arrest Care                                               Check

          Oxygenation and ventilation

          Measure oxygenation and target normoxemia 94%-99% (or child's normal/appropriate oxygen saturation).           

          Measure and target Paco2 appropriate to the patient's underlying condition and limit exposure to severe hypercapnia or hypocapnia. 

          Hemodynamic monitoring

          Set specific hemodynamic goals during post-cardiac arrest care and review daily.                               

          Monitor with cardiac telemetry.                                                                                

          Monitor arterial blood pressure.                                                                               

          Monitor serum lactate, urine output, and central venous oxygen saturation to help guide therapies.             

          Use parenteral fluid bolus with or without inotropes or vasopressors to maintain a systolic blood pressure greater than the fifth percentile for age and sex. 

          Targeted temperature management (TTM)

          Measure and continuously monitor core temperature.                                                             

          Prevent and treat fever immediately after arrest and during rewarming.                                         

          If patient is comatose apply TTM (32�C-34�C) followed by (36�C-37.5�C) or only TTM (36�C-37.5�C).              

          Prevent shivering.                                                                                             

          Monitor blood pressure and treat hypotension during rewarming.                                                 

          Neuromonitoring

          If patient has encephalopathy and resources are available, monitor with continuous electroencephalogram. 

          Treat seizures.                                                                                                

          Consider early brain imaging to diagnose treatable causes of cardiac arrest.                                   

          Electrolytes and glucose

          Measure blood glucose and avoid hypoglycemia.                                                                  

          Maintain electrolytes within normal ranges to avoid possible life-threatening arrhythmias.                     

          Sedation

          Treat with sedatives and anxiolytics.                                                                          

          Prognosis

          Always consider multiple modalities (clinical and other) over any single predictive factor.                    

          Remember that assessments may be modified by TTM or induced hypothermia.                                       

          Consider electroencephalogram in conjunction with other factors within the first 7 days after cardiac arrest.  

          Consider neuroimaging such as magnetic resonance imaging during the first 7 days.

===== PAGE 38 =====
Contents                                                                 Atrial Fibrillation and Wolff-Parkinson-White Syndrome

       Recent evidence: No survival benefit in rate vs rhythm control                                                                  * Irregularly irregular

          NON-TRAUMATIC BACK PAIN

                                           GENERAL APPROACH                                                                  RED FLAGS

                                         One of the most common complaints in ED                 � New pain at extremes of age (< 20 or >50)
                        NB! History and examination more useful than special investigations.     � History of malignancy
                                                                                                 � Constant, non-mechanical pain, or worse lying down/at night
 � Majority of NON-TRAUMA cases are benign musculoskeletal lower back pain                       � ANY neurology - weakness, numbness, paraesthesias etc.
 � Physical exam concerning features:                                                            � Bilateral symptoms
                                                                                                 � Immune suppression (HIV, DM, steroids, immune suppressants)
          � Motor: arm/leg weakness, especially bilateral                                        � IV drug use
          � Sensory: saddle anaesthesia, paraesthesia, sensory level                             � Bowel/bladder function change, erectile dysfunction
          � Reflexes: any abnormal reflexes, including babinski                                  � Fever, night sweats, weight loss
          � Sphincter dysfunction: decreased rectal tone, urinary retention/incontinence         � Anticoagulant use e.g. warfarin
          � Midline spinal tenderness, deformity, swelling, fluctuance
          � Systemic: wasting, fever, lymphadenopathy, other pathologic fractures                                NON-SPINE RELATED CAUSES
 � Imaging: Spinal x-rays are neither sensitive nor specific
 � Back pain with new neurology, or suspicion for epidural abscess requires urgent MRI         � VASCULAR: AAA, aortic dissection, renal artery
 � Lab workup for underlying condition as indicated                                                 dissection/thrombosis

                                        SPINE RELATED CAUSES                                   � RENAL: renal colic, pyelonephritis, perinephric abscess, prostatitis
                                                                                               � GIT: pancreatitis, PUD, cholecystitis
� Infectious: Spinal epidural abscess, vertebral osteomyelitis, infectious discitis, TB spine  � ANTICOAGULANTS: retroperitoneal haemorrhage
� Oncological: Pathological fracture, bone or epidural metastases, spinal tumours              � GYNAE: PID, rarely ectopic pregnancy. Lower back pain is common
� Haematological: Spinal epidural haematoma - anticoagulants, recent lumbar
                                                                                                    in PREGNANCY
    puncture/epidural anaesthesia/spinal surgery
� Neurological: Cauda equina syndrome, disc herniation causing cord compression                                            DISPOSITION
� Degenerative: Spondylolysis/spondylolisthesis/OA with radiculopathy                          � Low risk patients: Non-specific pain < 6 weeks with no

                                        SPECIFIC MANAGEMENT                                        red flags, monoradiculopathy/sciatica - DC with simple
    � Epidural abscess: Antibiotics - Consider Ceftriaxone, Cefazolin or Vancomycin                analgesics and physio/spine OPD follow up
    � Suspected metastases: Dexamethasone 10mg IV stat, urgent MRI                             � High risk patients - manage underlying cause, admit to
                                                                                                   appropriate subspecialty once stable

             LUND AND BROWDER CHART

                                                                  Escharotomy

                                     Indications
                                     Circumferential burn with vascular compromise
                                     � pain, paraesthesia, decreased pulse, pale limb

                                     Procedure
                                     * Procedural sedation
                                     * Incisions along axial lines, medial & lateral of limbs & torso
                                     * Incisions 1-2cm through into normal tissues above & below the

                                       burn
                                     * Cut deep enough to view subcutaneous tissue - may be required

                                       to breach fascia
                                     * Thereafter maintain haemostasis with pressure
                                     * Avoid the following areas:

                                               - Ulnar nerve - posterior to medial epicondyle
                                               - Common peroneal nerve - head of fibula
                                               - Posterior tibial artery & nerve - posterior to medial malleolus
                                     * Hands
                                               - Cutaneous incisions strictly mid-axial lateral or medial to
                                                 avoid palmar & digital neurovasculature
                                               - Never cut palmar aspects or tips of fingers

          b-BLOCKER & CALCIUM-CHANNEL BLOCKER OVERDOSE

                                      CLINICAL FEATURES                                              HIGH DOSE INSULIN EUGLYCAEMIC THERAPY
 * Hypotension, bradycardia, hyperglycaemia, acidosis, CNS depression                                                         (HIET)

                                         ECG FEATURES                                         * Start with a 50ml 50% dextrose bolus
 * Sinus bradycardia, AV blocks, junctional bradycardia, ventricular bradycardia              * Insulin loading dose:

  NOTE:                                                                                                    � Actrapid 1 IU/kg (ie 60-80 units) IV stat
  � Propranolol: Also acts as a Na channel blocker - TCA-like features of OD                  * Infusion:
  � Sotalol: Blocks myocardial K channels - QT prolongation, Torsades
                                                                                                           � Actrapid 0.5 IU/kg/hr, titrate up every 30
                                  GENERAL APPROACH                                                              minutes to a max dose of 10 IU/kg/hr IV

  � Triage to RESUS AREA, even if patient initially appears well                              *Dextrose infusion:
  � Rapid ABC assessment, 2 IV lines, monitors, HGT, VBG, urine BHCG, ECG                                  � Maintenance @ 25g/hr
  � Correct abnormal electrolytes
  � Crystalloid fluid bolus 20mg/kg if hypotensive                                                     (50ml 50% dextrose/hr or 100ml 25% dextrose) via central
  � Comprehensive history and exam, including:                                                                            line to maintain euglycemia

          o Amount and time of ingestion, co-ingestants                                       * Monitor HGT every 20 minutes for 1st hour, then every 1
          o Comorbidities, chronic medications, symptoms prior to arrival                      hour
  � Document specific features of toxicity
  � Further investigations: FBC, UE, LFT, Paracetamol/Salicylate level, urine tox screen      * Monitor Potassium - replace if < 2.5 mmol

*Enhanced elimination: Consider activated charcoal 50g via NGT                                         � EF > 50%; SBP > 90mmHg

     � HIET - see protocol                                                                             � Improved GCS

     � Consider ECMO if no response to HIET - specialist consultation                                                                              DISPOSITION

    CRITICAL CARE MANAGEMENT PRINCIPLES

                  GENERAL FOR ALL PATIENTS IN THE ED

                                   "FASTHUGS IN BED"

                            F - Fluid therapy and Feeding
                            A - Analgesia and Antibiotics (if indicated),

                                 Antiemetics (if indicated)
                            S - Sedation and Spontaneous Breathing Trial
                            T - Thromboprophylaxis

                                Clexane� 0.5mg/kg OD SC;
                                Clexane� 0.25mg/kg OC SC if renal dysfunction (GFR<30)
                            H - Head up position (30-45�) if intubated
                            U - Ulcer prophylaxis
                                 Pantaloc� 40mg OD IV
                            G - Glucose control (Target: 5-10mmol/l)
                            S - Skin/eye care and suctioning
                            I - Indwelling catheters - are they needed?
                            N - Nasogastric tube
                            B - Bowel cares
                            E - Environment
                                 e.g. temperature control, appropriate surroundings in
                                 delirium
                            D - De-escalation
                                 e.g. antibiotics, sedation, end of life care/decisions

                           NEUROPROTECTIVE MEASURES

                                                      (to prevent 2� brain injury)

                *Head up 30-45�
                *MAP > 90mmHg unless ICH then MAP > 75-80mmHg
                *Normocarbia (PCO2 35-40mmHg) � lower end of normal
                *Normoxia (PaO2 60-100mmHg) � avoid hypoxia; O2 Sats target > 95%
                *Normothermia (Temp < 37.5�C)
                *Normoglycaemia (Blood glucose target � 5-10mmol/l)
                *Correct any electrolyte abnormalities
                *Monitor for seizures
                  (no role for prophylactic anticonvulsant therapy unless 2� to trauma)
                *Sedation and analgesia to avoid coughing/gagging � avoid causing raised ICP
                *Consider Fentanyl premed when intubating to blunt sympathetic intubation response

          INITIAL LUNG PROTECTIVE VENTILATOR SETTINGS

          MODE       Volume Assist Control
          TV         6ml/kg (4-6ml/kg)
          I:E ratio  1:2
          RR         10 -12 bpm
          PEEP       8-12 (titrate to O2 saturations)
          Alarm      Pmax < 30
          FiO2       Titrate down rapidly acc to O2 saturations

                        VENTILATOR TROUBLESHOOTING

          * Displacement of ET tube
          * Obstruction of ET tube / mucous plug
          * Pneumothorax / pneumomediastinum
          * Equipment failure / disconnection
          * Suctioning required / stomach overdistension

                                           ALARMS

                (check that alarm settings and pressure cut-offs are set appropriately for the patient)

          PEAK PRESSURE
          � check ventilator tubing, ET tube, filter, bronchospasm, mucous plug, patient biting the tube

          PLATEAU PRESSURE
          � check for ARDS, lung contusion, pneumonia, oedema, pleural effusion, pneumothorax, breath

             stacking, right mainstem intubation, increased intra-abdominal pressure

          MINUTE VOLUME
          � Check respiratory rate, tidal volume, pressure support

          OXYGEN CONCENTRATION (FiO2)
          � Ensure ventilator connected to oxygen and � air

          RESPIRATORY RATE
          � Check for patient-ventilator dyssynchrony, patient agitation, pain, appropriate trigger, hypoxia,

             secretions in ET tube

          LOW PRESSURE
          � circuit disconnection, ET tube low cuff pressure/leaking cuff

          POWER ALARM
          � Check plugs and connections

          CRUSH INJURY

                   Causes                             Clinical Examination

          * Extensive soft tissue trauma    * Tramline bruising
               - Community assault          * Tense, oedematous, painful muscle groups
               - Sjambok injury             * Dark / tea-coloured urine
                                            * Circulatory shock
          * Snake bite
          * Compartment syndrome                           Investigations
          * Strenuous exercise
          * Hyperthermia                  * Blood gas - metabolic acidosis, � lactate
          * Electric injury               * U-dipstix - haemoglobin / protein
          * Burns (3rd & 4th degree)      * eFAST
          * Severe sepsis                 * Bloods: Creatinine kinase, myoglobin, U&E, CMP
          * Metabolic myopathies
          * Drug overdose

               - Isoniazid
               - Corticosteroids
               - Colchicine
               - Anti-malarial
               - Heroine
               - Zidovudine

                        ED Management

          * ABCD approach to resuscitation

          * Aggressive fluid administration
                   - Crystalloids (Balsol / Ringers Lactate)
                   - Rate: 15 - 30 ml/kg/hr

          * Monitor urinary output
                      - Aim for urinary output of 200 - 300 mls/hr

          * Correct Electrolyte Abnormalities

          * Indication for Mannitol
                    - urine output < 20mls/hr

          * Indications for NaHCO3 8.5% (1ml/kg)
                    - severe metabolic acidosis
                    - hyperkalaemia correction

          * Indications for referral for dialysis
                    - persistent acidosis
                    - refractory hyperkalaemia
                    - � urine output

          * Disposition
                    - Refer Trauma/Surgery depending on cause

                                                                                Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

                 HAEMATOLOGICAL EMERGENCIES

                              HAEMOPHILIA

                                         GENERAL

           � Haemophilia A = Factor VIII (80% of cases)
           � Haemophilia B = IX deficiency (20% of cases)
           � Acquired: malignancies, pregnancy, auto-immune (SLE, RA), idiopathic
           � Most patients will know their INTRINSIC FACTOR ACTIVITY %, and will

                know the type of factor they take.
                If unsure, assume 0%. Try to use what the patient is on.

                              CLASSIFICATION OF BLEEDS

          � MINOR BLEEDS
              Soft tissue, muscle, haemarthrosis, epistaxis �50% factor replacement

          � MAJOR BLEEDS
              CNS, GI, Neck/airway, major trauma �100% factor replacement

                      FACTOR REPLACEMENT CALCULATION                                                 HAEMOPHILIA A or B with INHIBITOR

          � VIII required = weight x 0.5 x (% activity desired - % intrinsic activity)    For individuals with inhibitors (antibodies to factor VIII or IX), treatment decisions
          � IX required = weight x 1 x (% activity desired - % intrinsic activity)        may be complicated.
                                                                                          The care of inhibitor patients should be urgently discussed with Haematology.
                        ADDITIONAL TREATMENT OPTIONS                                      LIFE OR LIMB THREATENING SCENARIOS
                                                                                          *Recombinant factor VIIa (rFVIIa) at 90�g/kg OR
          � DDAVP (0.3�g/kg IV in 30ml normal saline over 15-30min), FFP, PCC,            *Activated prothrombin complex concentrates (FEIBA) at 75-100u/kg
              cryoprecipitate - in consultation with Haematology, if factor not
              available                                                                                         Dr J Du Plessis / Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

          All other resuscitation measures still apply - treat the patient, not just the
                                               haemophilia!

                                    https://litfl.com

            Laboratory: FBC/ smear/retics/ U&E/ Clotting profile/ LFT/Blood cultures/CRP   - Allergic reaction
            *FBC & smear - Increased reticulocytes, low Hb, low Platelets, schiztocytes    - Catheter related complications
            *Haemolytic anaemia - Increased LDH and bilirubin (Direct Coombs - negative)  *Disposition
            *Coagulation studies - normal to mildly elevated D-dimer & fibrinogen         - Urgent Haematology referral
            Radiological: CXR, others as clinically indicated

                      HYPOKALAEMIA

                           K+ < 3.5mmol/l

              BOTH Potassium & Magnesium need to be replaced simultaneouly in hypokalaemia
                    (Mg2+ activates the Na+- K+ ATPase pump to promote cellular uptake of K+)

               40 mmol KCl + 2g MgSO4 in 200 ml
                 0.9% Normal Saline over 4 hours
          (via infusion pump / dial-a-flow @ 50 ml/hr)

                        LIFE-THREATENING HYPOKALAEMIA
                           (cardiac arrest / impending arrest)

          20 mEq/mmol KCl (10ml) can be administered @ 1ml/min then
                                   re-check potassium level

HYPERNATRAEMIA                                                          Definition: Na+ > 145mmol/l
                                                                  Correction only needed if >150mmol/l
             Investigations:                  Symptoms: Nausea, Vomiting, dehydration, lethargy, weakness, muscle
      Serum - U&E, sOsmolality,                   irritability (twitching, seizures), ataxia, drowsiness, stupor, coma
       Urine - uNa, uOsmolality                         Pathophysiology: water depletion and/or solute excess

          Equations: Calculated serum osmolality = (2 x sNa+) + glucose + urea
                      Free water deficit: 0.6 x Weight(kg) x [(sNa+ - 140)/140]
                        5DW or half normal saline infusion rate: Use MDCalc

                                                   General Approach if critically Ill:
- ABC's, Monitors, IV, ECG, urine catheter
- Shock/Dehydration � fluid resuscitation 1st priority using isotonic solutions e.g. Balsol / RL (relatively hypotonic compared to serum)
- Estimate fluid deficit and infuse 5% dextrose water or half-normal saline over 48 hours.
- Aim to correct Na+ by 10-12mmol/l in 24hrs (Not more than 0.5mmol/l/hour) � DO NOT CORRECT TOO FAST FOR RISK OF CEREBRAL OEDEMA.
- Tap water can also be given orally or via NGT.
- Investigate and treat cause

                                               Causes of Hypernatraemia

                                                                   Hypernatraemia

                                                              Extra-Cellular Fluid Status

Hypovolaemia/Dehydration                      Isovolaemia                                   Hypervolaemia/Oedema

                 Na+ <<< H2O Loss                       Mild to Moderate H2O Loss              Na+ >>> H20 Retention
       1) Renal Losses (uNa+ > 20 mmol/l)        1) Renal H2O Loss (uOsm < 700mOsm/l)       1) Mineralocorticoid Excess:
                                               Diabetes Insipidus (central or nephrogenic)
              Diuretics eg furosemide         2) Extra-renal H2O Loss (uOsm > 700mOsm/l)         Cushing Syndrome
                  Osmotic Diuresis                                                              Hyperaldosteronsim

    2) Extra-renal Losses (uNa+ < 10 mmol/l)                                                        2) Exogenous:
      Sweating, Diarrhea, Vomiting, Burns                                                         Salt water intake

      Replace H2O AND Na+:                                Replace H20:                       Remove Na+ AND H2O:
Initially Isotonic fluid e.g. Ringers               5DW or Oral free water                  Diuretics e.g. furosemide
                                                     If Diabetes Insipidus:                  and replace losses with
 Lactate until euvolaemic, then               Central: Treat with DDAVP* 1-2�g              5DW or oral free water to
     5DW or Oral Free water                                                                 achieve negative balance
                                                             IM/SC/IV                       (Urine Output > Input by
                                               Nephrogenic: Fluid Restriction,
                                                                                                   1-2 l per day)
                                                         DDAVP*, HCTZ

            DIABETES INSIPIDUS (DI) Is the passage of large volumes (>3 L/24 hr) of dilute urine. It has the following 2 major forms:
                      Central: (neurogenic, pituitary, or neurohypophyseal) characterized by decreased secretion of ADH
                      Nephrogenic: characterized by decreased ability to concentrate urine because of resistance to ADH action in the kidney
                      *DDAVP = Desmopressin (antidiuretic effects at vasopressin receptors)

            HYPONATRAEMIA                                                       Definition: Na < 135mmol/l
                                                             Usually Asymptomatic unless acute or severe (<120mmol/l)
                           Investigations:                   Symptoms: Nausea, Vomiting, CNS Dysfunction (headache,
          Serum - U&E, sOsmolality, Gluc, Lipids, Prot
                                                                       irritability, lethargy, seizures, coma) death
                    Urine - uNa+, uOsmolality                    Pathophysiology: Solute depletion or solute dilution

                             Classification based on serum Osmolality (N= 272-295mOsm):
                   If Hypertonic: Mannitol, osmotic diuresis, hyperglycemia (DKA, HHS), alcoholism
If Isotonic: Pseudohyponatremia- Artefactual (lab error), hyperlipidemia, hyperproteinemia (Multiple myeloma)

                                               If Hypotonic: True Hyponatraemia

                         Equations: Calculated serum osmolality = (2 x Na+) + glucose + urea
                              Corrected Na+ for hyperglycaemia: Na+ + [2.4(Glucose - 5.4)/5.4]
                                     Hypertonic / Normal saline infusion rate: Use MDCalc

              True Hyponatraemia                   General Approach if critically Ill:
                                               -ABC's, Monitors, IV, ECG, urine catheter

           -If pronounced CNS dysfunction (usually Na+ <110): Give 3% hypertonic Saline: 2mls/kg over 60min, aim to
          raise Na+ to 120mmol/l. Once at 120mmol/l, slow rate of Na+ increase to maximum rate of 0.5mmol/l/hr (NB:

                     Maximum increase 12mmol/l per 24hrs) *if no Hypertonic saline available, Sodium Bicarbonate (1-2mls/kg) can be used

          -DO NOT CORRECT TOO FAST for risk of pontine myelinolysis. If low GCS with Na >120mmol, search for other
                                                            cause of coma or seizures.

                                           Approach based on cause (once stable - Na+ > 120):

        HYPERTHERMIA                                             � Clinical features: Decreased GCS, agitation, sweating (no sweating in heat stroke)

                      DEFINITIONS
                                                                      and VF can occur
      Minor heat illness                                         � Triage to RESUS AREA, CENTRAL TEMPERATURE MONITORING

      Heat exhaustion                                            � Correct electrolyte abnormalities & HGT

           LABETALOL

           Indications
               � Aortic dissection
               � Hypertensive encephalopathy
               � Stroke - ischaemic (if patient qualifies for thrombolysis)
               � Stroke - haemorrhagic (in order to prevent re-bleed)

                    TREATMENT TARGET

            Within 1st hour: Reduce MAP by 25%, DBP not less
                                     than 110 mmHg

               � Start with 20 mg bolus (4ml) of Labetalol
               � Add remaining 16 ml (80 mg) + a 2nd vial (100 mg) of

                  Labetalol to 200 ml 0.9% Normal Saline (0.76 mg/ml)
               � Start infusion via infusion pump or dial-a-flow at 80 ml/hr
               � Increase by 10 ml/hr every 5 minutes until BP target

                  reached
               � Maximum 160 ml/hr (2 mg/min)

   Consider INH toxicity in all refractory seizures, especially if on TB Rx/prophylaxis     � KNOWN AMOUNT INGESTED

� Clinical features: Depletes Vit B6, causing decreased GABA - seizures, lactic acidosis                 max 5g total or 70mg/kg in children.
� ECG Features: No specific features, sinus tachycardia
� Bloodgas: HAGMA, severe lactic acidosis                                                   � UNKNOWN AMOUNT INGESTED
                                                                                                     � Give 5g total
� Triage to RESUS AREA, even if patient initially appears well                                       � Repeat after 10 - 20 minutes if no response
� Terminate seizures with Benzodiazepines (high doses may be required).

    See Status Epilepticus protocol, but avoid anti-epileptic agents.                       � Crush tablets and administer via NGT once airway is

� Early airway management - will require intubation prior to Pyridoxine administration          protected
� Correct abnormal electrolytes, consider other causes of seizures                          � Call pharmacy early to request enough stock, as it is

       � Amount and time of ingestion, co-ingestants

                       ACUTE LIVER FAILURE IN THE ED

     Definition

      Rapid development of severe acute liver injury characterised by impaired synthetic function
      (INR � 1.5) and complicated by the development of encephalopathy, in a person without a history of liver
      disease or who had well-compensated liver disease.

     Causes (DAVES)

           � Drugs (paracetamol, TB drugs, ARVs, NSAIDs, traditional medicine, illicit drugs)
           � Alcohol
           � Viral (Hep A, B, C, E; EBV; CMV; HSV; HZV, ParvoB19)
           � Extras (acute fatty liver of pregnancy, HELLP, ischaemic necrosis, vascular, metabolic, autoimmune,

               Wilson's disease, Budd-Chiari, idiopathic, heatstroke, Reye's, malignancy
           � Sepsis

Clinical features of Acute Liver Failure              Clinical signs on examination of ALF

                       History                       � Jaundice
                                                     � RUQ tenderness
    � Date of onset of symptoms                      � Hepatomegaly
    � Travel history                                 � Splenomegaly
    � Drug use - prescription and illicit            � Ascites
    � Alcohol use                                    � Evidence of bleeding
    � Herbal or traditional medication use           � Evidence of infection
    � Target symptoms consistent with the            � Asterixis (hepatic flap)
                                                     � Evidence of encephalopathy
         presenting complication
    � Exposure to a viral illness (sexual contacts,

         body piercing)

Grading of Hepatic Encephalopathy (HE)

Grade                Intellectual Function             Neuromuscular Function

I      Impaired attention, irritable, depressed, personality Tremor, apraxia, incoordination

       changes

II     Drowsy, poor memory, behavioural changes, sleep Asterixis, ataxia, slow slurred

       disturbances                                    speech

III    Confusion, somnolence, disorientation, amnesia  Hypoactive reflexes, clonus,

                                                       nystagmus, muscle rigidity

IV     Stupor, coma                                    Dilated pupils, decerebtrate
                                                       posture, oculocephalic reflex

                                                       Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

===== PAGE 93 =====
Contents

     Complications and Clinical Manifestations

      Patients in ALF tend to present to the ED with complications that may include any of the following listed in
      the box.

                       Complications and clinical manifestations of acute liver failure
                 � Neurological

                         � Hepatic encephalopathy
                         � Cerebral oedema
                         � Seizures
                         � Raised ICP
                 � Coagulopathy and bleeding
                 � Metabolic
                         � Hypoglycaemia
                         � Hyponatraemia
                         � Hypokalaemia
                         � Hypophosphataemia
                         � Lactic acidosis
                         � Hyperammonaemia
                 � Infection

                             � Septicaemia
                             � Peritonitis
                             � Pneumonia
                             � Urinary tract sepsis

                 � Respiratory
                         � Hypoxia
                         � Aspiration
                         � ARDS (non-cardiogenic pulmonary oedema)

                 � Cardiovascular
                         � Hypotension
                         � Hypertension (raised ICP)

                 � Renal failure

                                          General Management Approach Goals

           � Identification of the aetiology of the ALF whenever possible and initiation of specific treatment
           � Supportive and symptomatic management of ALF with timely transfer to a critical care unit

         � Early consultation with Liver Transplant specialists

     Emergency Department Management

     Immediate management
         � Resuscitate and stabilize ABCs, blood glucose, oxygen if hypoxic and establish IV access.

        � Intubate and ventilate if unresponsive (normal glucose) from HE (RSI to prevent aspiration)
        � Nurse head up 30�

                                                                                                                                                  Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

===== PAGE 94 =====
Contents

        � Hypotension should first be addressed by correcting hypovolaemia with crystalloids.
        � Commence inotropes (Adrenaline) if needed.
        � Glucose monitoring 2 hourly
        � Stop all hepatotoxic and nephrotoxic drugs
        � Avoid sedatives - give short-acting benzodiazepines if needed

     Specific management

      Metabolic disturbances

         � Correct hypoglycaemia, hyponatraemia, hypokalaemia and other electrolyte abnormalities.

      Cerebral oedema, seizures, raised intracranial pressure & hepatic encephalopathy

         � Exclude other causes of a depressed level of consciousness: hypoglycaemia, intracranial bleed/head

               injury, etc

         � Lactulose 10mls TDS po (reduces ammonia thus reducing cerebral oedema)
         � Seizures: short-acting benzodiazepines (Midazolam 0.1mg/kg) to terminate seizures.

               If seizures continue load with phenytoin.

         � Raised ICP: Mannitol (0.5-1g/kg) and aim to maintain serum osmolarity < 320 mOsm/L.

               If renal failure is present be vigilant for fluid overload.

         � HE: consider 30% hypertonic saline at 5-20ml/hr to maintain sodium levels of 145-155 to prevent

               raised intracranial pressure.
      Coagulopathy and bleeding

         � Vitamin K 10mg IV as a slow bolus
         � FFPs 15ml/kg only if the patient is actively bleeding, undergoing surgery or requires a procedure.
         � Platelet count < 50: patient actively bleeding or undergoing surgery give platelets at 10ml/kg. Check

               platelet count at 1hr and 24hrs after infusion.
      Infection

         � Identify and treat any suspected infection.
         � There is no role for prophylactic antibiotics.
         � Suspected Herpes or Varicella Zoster: Acyclovir 5-10mg/kg q8hrly IV

      Indications for N-Acetylcysteine (NAC)

         � NAC is used in the majority of cases involving drugs/toxins.
         � DO NOT withhold even if the ingestion was 48-72 hrs prior to presentation.
         � Recommend administration until there is evidence of improved hepatic function - resolving HE,

               improving coagulopathy INR < 1.5 and declining transaminases.

         � Administer NAC if suspect paracetamol or drug-induced ALF
         � Mushroom poisoning: NAC with Penicillin G
         � NAC administration dose:

               150mg/kg in 200ml 5% Dextrose IV over 1 hr followed by
               50mg/kg in 500ml 5% Dextrose over 4 hrs followed by
               100mg/kg in 1L 5% Dextrose over 16 hrs
               If hepatic function not resolved, continue with: 150mg/kg in 1L 5% Dextrose over 24 hrs

                                                                                                                                                    Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

     Emergency Department Investigations

      Bedside

         � Glucose
         � Urine dipstix and U-bHCG

      Laboratory
           � Blood gas (arterial if the respiratory system is affected)
           � FBC, U&E, CMP, LFT, INR, plasma ammonia level, paracetamol, blood culture
           � Compact - Type and screen

      Radiological
           � CXR

         � Abdominal ultrasound

      Tailor other investigations to clinical findings & to rule in/rule out other differential diagnoses.

     Disposition
         � ALL patients with ALF should be referred to a physician for further management.
         � If intubated - admit/transfer to ICU
         � Early consultation with Liver Transplant specialists

           NON-INVASIVE VENTILATION (NIV)

                                              INDICATIONS

                              � Cardiogenic pulmonary oedema
                              � Acute exacerbation of COPD poorly responsive to

                                   initial treatment
                              � Pre-oxygenation for Rapid Sequence Intubation
                              � Severe pneumonia (preoxygenation)
                              � Neuromuscular respiratory failure

                                         CONTRAINDICATIONS

                                 � Cardiac arrest
                                 � Respiratory arrest
                                 � � LOC
                                 � Uncooperative patient (agitated/somnolent)
                                 � High aspiration risk
                                 � Haemodynamic instablility
                                 � Craniofacial trauma / burns to face
                                 � Non-fitting mask
                                 � Indications for intubation
                                 �

                    HOW TO PERFORM NIV

          1. Manage patient in the Resuscitation Area
          2. Attach patient to monitors: cardiac monitors, NIBP, pulse oximetry
          3. Sit patient up > 45�
          4. Explain the procedure to the patient and family
          5. Select the appropriate size mask for NIV

                   � Ensure a well-fitted mask with straps in place to allow a complete, good
                       seal with no air leak

          6. Ventilator
                   � CPAP mode / NIV mode
                   � Set a PEEP of 4cmH20
                              � Increase PEEP in increments of 2cmH20
                              � Monitor O2 saturation and haemodynamic status closely
                   � Set PSV initially on 0 cmH20
                   � Start with a Fi02 of 1 (100% Oxygen). Titrate according to O2 saturation

          7. Continuous monitoring, reassurance and check mask fit regularly
          8. Consider anxiolysis Ketamine 0.25mg/kg or Midazolam 1mg stat IV

          OXYGENATION - INCREASE PEEP
           VENTILATION - INCREASE PSV

                                                   Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

          PARACETAMOL OVERDOSE

                                        TOXIC DOSE
                              150mg/kg or > 10g in adults

                                   INITIAL ASSESSMENT & MANAGEMENT

          * Assess and support ABC's
          * Monitor glucose
          * Attempt to quantify the amount ingested & of any co-ingestants
          * Establish a timeframe from the time of ingestion to presentation
          * Presentation < 1 hour from ingestion

              � Activated charcoal 50g PO (not via NGT)
          * ECG - look for signs of co-ingestants e.g. prolonged QT
          * Laboratory investigations

              � Blood gas, U&E, salicylate level

             � Paracetamol levels (provided � 4 hours post ingestion)

              � No need for AST/ALT/INR unless > 12 hours post ingestion

              **Refer to Protocol: General management principles of a poisoned/overdose patient**

                        N-Acetylcysteine (NAC) Parvolex� / Paradote�

          * Use the Paracetamol Treatment Nomogram to establish need for antidote
          * If a high dosage is suspected, rather give the antidote to the patient
          * DO NOT withhold even if the ingestion was 48-72hrs prior to presentation

          * Administer NAC as follows:
              � 150mg/kg in 200ml 5% Dextrose IV over 1 hour followed by
              � 50mg/kg in 500ml 5% Dextrose IV over 4 hours followed by
              � 100mg/kg in 1L 5% Dextrose IV over 16 hours

          NAC CAUTIONS/SIDE-EFFECTS                                           Start treatment as soon as possible
          *Anaphylaxis                                                                       if indicated,
          *Vomiting

Scorpion envenomation

Scorpion stings may cause life-threatening envenomation. Species identification is difficult, but a useful guide for the
clinician is that scorpions with thick tails and slender pincers are more venomous than those with thin tails and large
pincers. Scorpions are mainly nocturnal and active during the summer months. Apart from local pain and occasional
mild local inflammation, most scorpion stings are relatively harmless. In South Africa, only two species of the
Parabuthus genus have been associated with severe envenomation: Parabuthus granulatus and Parabuthus
transvaalicus. (See Figures 1 and 2 for description and geographical distribution)

Clinical features of severe envenomation (scorpionism):

 Immediate, severe local pain

 Generalised paraesthesia and hyperaesthesia

 Muscle pain and cramps

 Tremors and fasciculations

 Involuntary movements

 Agitation                                                       Figure 1: Parabuthus granulatus

 Visual disturbances

 Hypertension, tachycardia and cardiac arrhythmias

 General weakness - unable to walk, difficulty in breathing,

          dysphagia and drooling

In most cases, the degree of severity is evident within 15 - 60  Figure 2: Parabuthus transvaalicus
minutes, but in adults, systemic symptoms and signs may be
delayed for 8 hours.

Children may present with a unique form of restlessness marked by crying and thrashing which is an indication of severe
systemic envenomation. Bulbar paralysis, accompanied by respiratory failure, is more frequent in children and the
primary cause of death.

Differential diagnoses to consider in the absence of a history of a scorpion sting, include neurotoxic spider bites, snake
bites, cholinesterase inhibitor toxicity, drug overdose, Guillian-Barre syndrome and tetanus.

Management:

 Scorpion antivenom should be given to all patients with symptoms and signs of severe envenomation.
         o Standard dose is 5 - 10 mL intravenously for both children and adults, diluted in a small volume of
              normal saline and given over 15 minutes.
         o It may take 2 - 6 hours to reach its maximum effect.
         o An additional dose of 5 mL may be administered after 6 hours if response to first dose is inadequate.
         o The patients should be kept under observation for 6 - 12 hours after antivenom administration.

 Pain management:
         o If simple analgesia is ineffective, infiltrate the sting site with local anaesthetic.
         o Opioids are relatively ineffective and increase the risk of respiratory depression.
         o Generalised muscle cramps can be alleviated temporarily (for about 20 - 30 minutes) by IV
              administration of calcium gluconate 10% (Adults 10 mL and children 0.5 mL/kg).

 Support cardiorespiratory function as needed.
 Do NOT give antihistamines and steroids routinely.
 Tetanus toxoid if required.

Sources:
     www.afritox.co.za
     Muller et al, Scorpion sting in southern Africa: diagnosis and management. CME 2012; 30(10):362-382.

                                       NEW DEFINITIONS

          *SEPSIS:
          Life-threatening organ dysfunction due to a dysregulated host response to an infection

                   Clinical Criteria: Organ dysfunction = increase of 2 SOFA score

          *SEPTIC SHOCK:
          Sepsis + (despite adequate fluid resus)

                   : Persistent hypotension requiring vasopressor MAP >65
                   : Lactate >2

                       HAVE TO HAVE A HIGH INDEX OF CLINICAL SUSPICION
          NB: Young patients may compensate BP till late and have isolated raised lactate
          NB: Elderly may have isolated hypotension and very non-specific lactate levels
          NB Subgroups: Immuno-compromised, DM, Elderly, Pregnant females, Paediatrics

===== PAGE 126 =====
Contents

      ARVD (ARRHYTHMOGENIC RIGHT VENTRICULAR DYSPLASIA)
      Fibro-fatty replacement of the right ventricular myocardium
      Epsilon wave (ARROW - most specific finding, seen in 30% of patients)
      T wave inversion in V1-3 (85% of patients)
      Prolonged S-wave upstroke of 55ms in V1-3 (95% of patients)
      Localised QRS widening of 110ms in V1-3
      Paroxysmal episodes of ventricular tachycardia with LBBB morphology

      DILATED CARDIOMYOPATHY
      Left Atrial Enlargement may progress to atrial fibrillation.
      Bi-atrial enlargement
      LVH or Bi-ventricular enlargement
      LBBB (RBBB can also occur).
      Left Axis Deviation
      Poor R-Wave Progression with QS complexes in V1-4 ("pseudo-infarction" pattern).
      Frequent ventricular ectopicy or ventricular bigeminy (seen with severe DCM).
      Ventricular dysrhythmias (VT / VF).

===== PAGE 127 =====
Contents

      WOLFF-PARKINSON-WHITE SYNDROME (OR OTHER PRE-EXCITATION SYNDROMES)
      Shortened PR
      Prone to tachydysrhythmias (AVRT)

      HYPERTROPHY

      INFARCTION
      *See ECG Changes in ACS Protocol

               AGITATION IS A SYMPTOM, NOT A DIAGNOSIS
          IT IS VITAL TO FIND THE UNDERLYING CAUSE URGENTLY

                             FIRST 5 MINUTES                                                         NEXT 10 MINUTES

             RESUSCITATE AND EXCLUDE LIFE THREATENING EMERGENCIES                              INVESTIGATE AND CORRECT REVERSIBLE FACTORS

          � Apply monitors, including capnography                           � Bloodgas - correct electrolytes, acidosis
          � 2 large-bore IV lines                                           � Head to toe examination
          � Assess and treat:
                                                                                Toxidromes, trauma, track marks
              Hypoxia - O2                                                      Systemic and neurological exam
              Hyperthermia - core temperature, start cooling measures       � ECG - toxicology features, ischaemia, arrhythmias, QT interval
              Hypoglycemia - HGT check, 50% dextrose                        � Urine for dipstix, bHCG and tox screen

          � ABC management as indicated                                     � POCUS as indicated - fluid status, diagnostic aid

              compromised                                                       recent illness/activities/trauma, previous episodes, baseline functional

                               TRIAGE

           � From the French word "trier", literally means: "to sort".
           � The aim is to bring "the greatest good to the greatest number of people".
           � This is achieved through prioritising limited resources to achieve the greatest possible benefit.
           � Patients are sorted with a scientific triage scale in order of urgency.
           � The end result is that the patient with the greatest need is helped first.

                 CMJAH EMERGENCY DEPARTMENT
                            TRIAGE PROCESS

           � ALL NEW PATIENTS AND ALL THOSE BROUGHT TO THE HOSPITAL BY AMBULANCE ARE TRIAGED.
           � There are 2 triage areas

                    o Emergency Department (ED) Area 165
                    o Area 160 24 hours - Stable walk-in and referred patients
           � The South African Triage Scale, a well-validated triage tool is used.
           � The process involves taking a brief history and performing patient vital signs.
           � Depending on the nature of the complaint, the vital signs of a patient, and the presence or absence
               of discriminators, patients are triaged into one of 4 categories i.e. red, orange, yellow and green.
           � Patients are also continuously informed and educated that they are not seen on a first come first
               served basis but rather according to priority and urgency.
           � The targeted time to treat for the various categories are:
                    o Red - Immediate
                    o Orange - < 10 minutes
                    o Yellow - < 60 minutes
                    o Green - < 240 minutes
               However, note that these times may vary depending on the number of patients in each category as
               well as the number of doctors on duty.
           � Some patients, provided they are stable, may be redirected/down referred using the triage form,
               and without having to open a file, to more appropriate facilities to prevent overburdening the
               hospital with cases that can be managed at a secondary/district/clinic level.
           � If a patient requires an ED consultation but is not mobile or does not have an escort who is able to
               open the file on their behalf, the relevant clerk should be alerted to come to the ED for patient
               particulars and open a file.
           � For stable, mobile patients and those with escorts, files are to be opened at the administrative
               counters
           � Arranged transfers in from other facilities are to be triaged in ED:
               UNSTABLE: ED doctors to assist with resuscitation and accepting discipline to assess and arrange
               disposition.
               STABLE: patient to be sent directly to the respective disciplines ward for work up and further
               management.

                                                                      Dr R Gihwala / Prof F Motara CMJAH Emergency Department Oct 2020

===== PAGE 138 =====
Contents

           CMJAH EMERGENCY DEPARTMENT
                        TRIAGE POLICY

      � ALL patients presenting to the Emergency Department will
         be triaged according to the South African Triage Score
         (SATS).

      � Patients must still be triaged even if the hospital is on
         "Ambulance Divert". Patients need to be confirmed stable
         prior to being diverted to an alternate hospital.

      � If a patient is triaged RED or ORANGE, nurses are to
         immediately ensure that a doctor who is physically present
         in the Emergency Department is notified immediately.

      � Arranged transfers in from other facilities are to be triaged
         in ED:
         UNSTABLE: ED doctors to assist with resuscitation and
         accepting discipline to assess and arrange disposition.
         STABLE: patient to be sent directly to the respective
         disciplines ward for work up and further management.

                                                                     Dr R Gihwala / Prof F Motara CMJAH Emergency Department Oct 2020

                   TRICYCLIC ANTIDEPRESSANT OVERDOSE

                                            GENERAL APPROACH                                                                              ECG FEATURES

               � Triage to RESUS AREA, even if patient initially appears well                                    � Na channel blocking agent
               � Rapid ABC assessment, 2 IV lines, monitors, HGT, VBG, urine BHCG                                � Sinus tachycardia
               � Correct electrolyte abnormalities                                                               � Prolonged PR interval
               � Comprehensive history and examination, including:                                               � Interventricular conduction delay - QRS > 100 ms

                          � Amount and time of ingestion, co-ingestants                                                    � QRS > 100ms predictive of seizures

               � Document specific features of TCA OD:                                                           � Prolonged QT interval
                          � Sedation/coma/seizures                                                               � RBBB
                          � Metabolic acidosis                                                                   � Right axis deviation of the terminal QRS
                          � Broad complex tachydysrhythmias, hypotension                                                   � Terminal R wave > 3mm in aVR (tall R wave)
                          � Anticholinergic syndrome                                                                       � R/S ratio > 0.7 in aVR
                          � ECG changes

             � Further investigations: FBC, U&E, LFT, Paracetamol/Salicylate level, urine tox
                                                                                                              � Hypertonic saline 3% 100ml boluses if Sodabic unavailable
                     screen                                                                                   � 3RD line treatment:

                                                                                                              � Consider Magnesium Sulphate if significant QT prolongation
� Significant overdose > 10 mg/kg                                                                             � Consider Intralipid in consultation with GIT/ICU

                                                                                                              � Contraindicated drugs:
          � Indications: pH < 7.1, QRS > 100ms, arrhythmias, seizures, hypotension
          � Repeat every 5 minutes until BP improves and QRS complexes narrow                                      Type 1a antiarrhythmics e.g. Procainamide; Amiodarone; b-blockers
          � If arrhythmias occur, cardioversion is unlikely to be successful, continue Sodabic boluses until
                                                                                                                                            DISPOSITION
                rhythm normalizes
          � If seizures occur, Sodabic is the 1st line treatment                                                            � GIT consult
� Intubate early for airway protection and hyperventilation - BEWARE WORSENING ACIDOSIS                                     � Will likely require ICU/High care

                ULTRASOUND GUIDED FEMORAL NERVE BLOCK

                            INDICATIONS

          * Neck of femur fractures
          * Femoral fractures
          * Knee and patellar injuries
          * Anterior thigh laceration, abscess drainage etc.
          * Any painful procedure of the area innervated by

            the femoral nerve
                                                                                                                                X

                      THIS BLOCK WILL ANAESTHETISE
                          � Anterior thigh
                          � Most of the femur
                          � Knee
                          � Skin on the medial aspect
                              of the lower leg

HOW TO PERFORM AN ULTRASOUND GUIDED FEMORAL
                          NERVE BLOCK

* Place the patient in a supine position
* Clean and drape
* Place the linear transducer in the inguinal crease to locate the femoral

  nerve (visualized lateral to the pulsatile common femoral artery).
* On visualization of the nerve, insert the needle just below the inguinal

  ligament and lateral to the artery.
* Aspirate before injecting to avoid injecting into a vessel.
* Infiltrate 20ml Marcaine (2-3mg/kg)
* Successful block can be expected when local anaesthetic is seen

  circumferentially around the nerve

                                                                            Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

       UPPER GI BLEED IN THE ED

                   HISTORY                                            EXAMINATION

                                               * Digital rectal examination to assess for melena
    � Abdominal pain                           * Look for signs of liver disease
    � Haematemesis                             * Signs of acute abdomen suggest perforation
    � Melena
    � Haematochezia                                                 INVESTIGATIONS
    � Syncope (especially in elderly)              * NB! initial Hb may be normal

    � Alcohol abuse                                * LFT only if indicated
    � NSAIDs                                       * CXR erect � assess for free air under diaphragm
    � Antiplatelet / anticoagulant therapy         * POCUS to assess volume status
    � Previous GI bleed

                               MANAGEMENT

* ABC approach to initial resuscitation
* 2 large bore IV lines � Give warm Balsol or Ringers Lactate (10mls/kg)
* Keep NPO
* Transfuse if:

             � Haemodynamic instability despite 1 crystalloid fluid bolus
             � Hb < 9 in a high risk patient i.e. elderly; coronary artery disease
             � Hb < 7 in a low risk patient
             � Avoid over transfusion if the bleeding may be 2� varices
*If haemodynamically unstable � Give FFP (10-20mls/kg)
*Pantoprazole (Pantoloc�)
             � Loading dose 80mg
             � Consider Infusion: 200mg in 200mls N/S @ 8 mls/hr
*Consider Sengstaken-Blakemore Tube for variceal bleeds
*Notify surgeons ASAP
*NB - no role for TXA in UGIB

                     DISPOSITION                               BLATCHFORD SCORE

Discharge only if patient meets ALL criteria:  * Urea < 6.5 mmol/l
         � Stable                              * Hb > 13 (males) ; > 12 (females)
         � No witnessed haematemesis in ED     * Systolic BP > 110 mmHg
         � Blatchford Score = 0                * Pulse < 100 bpm
                                               * Absence of melena, syncope, cardiac failure
Discharged patients should follow up at SOPD
                                                or liver disease

                                                     Dr R Gihwala CMJAH Emergency Department June 2020

                              ABDOMINAL AORTIC ANEURYSM (AAA)                                                      HIGH INDEX OF SUSPICION IS KEY!

� History: Sudden onset abdo/flank/back/groin/sciatic pain. Syncope. Hypovolaemic                       ALL VASCULAR EMERGENCIES ARE EXTREMELY TIME
     shock/Haemodynamic instability if leaking/ruptured. Lower limb ischaemia/cyanosis                                 SENSITIVE - DO NOT DELAY!

� Physical: Pulsatile abdominal mass (50% of cases). Unexplained hypotension/tachycardia.                       CRITICAL AND ACUTE LIMB ISCHAEMIA
